## [v05.32r] — 2026-09-09 10:07:11 PM EST

> **Prompt:** "I hard refreshed and can see the bold text now. Add the first-load staleness check."

### Added

- **`Profiler.html` v01.84w → v01.85w — a first-load staleness check, inside the existing `PROJECT OVERRIDE: cache-busting reload` block.** On the first poll the page compares `html.version.txt` against its own `<meta name="build-version">`; when the version file is ahead, the document it is running predates the deploy the version file describes, and `ovReloadFresh()` fires once to replace it. A new `ovDocVersion()` reads the meta tag back, normalised to the `parseVersionTxt()` shape

### Changed

- **`CLAUDE.md` [PC-HTML-SOURCE] #3 amended** — the meta tag was documented as "informational only … never read by the polling logic", which is no longer true on a page carrying the override. The rule now separates the two questions the two artefacts answer, states the normalisation trap, and makes the loop guard and the null-safe read mandatory for any page adopting it
- **`CLAUDE.md` [PC-HTML-VERSION] #2 amended** — it claimed that "changing only html.version.txt still triggers a reload correctly". On an override page that now costs every visitor one wasted reload before the guard gives up, so the rule says to bump both in the same commit
- **`.claude/rules/html-pages.md` reconciled** — its build-version bullet asserted the meta tag "is never involved in the reload mechanism". Split into three bullets covering what each artefact is for, why the baseline path could not catch this case, and the two guards adoption requires

### Notes

- **The gap was structural, not a bug in `ovReloadFresh()`.** That helper fires when the version *changes while the page is open*; a page opened fresh **after** a deploy sees no change at all, because the first fetch takes the version file as its baseline unconditionally. A stale document therefore reported itself current and nothing fired. **This is exactly how the v01.84w bold fix appeared not to work** on a device whose pill already read v01.84w — the same symptom the override block's own comment records for v01.30w's import bar, which is why the fix belongs in that block
- **The normalisation trap is real and would have inverted the feature.** `parseVersionTxt()` strips the leading `v`, so `parsed.version` is `01.85w` while the meta tag carries `v01.85w`. Comparing them raw marks **every** load stale. Verified by test A below, which passes only because `ovDocVersion()` strips it too
- **The loop guard is the load-bearing part, because the two values are bumped by hand and can drift.** [PC-HTML-VERSION] #2 requires both, but a session that bumps `html.version.txt` and forgets the `<meta>` creates a **permanent** mismatch that no reload can fix. The guard records the target version in `sessionStorage` and allows **one** attempt per version per tab, then falls through and lets the document run stale rather than reloading forever
- **A missing meta tag disables the check rather than triggering it.** `ovDocVersion()` returns `null` when the tag is absent, so older copies and any page that has not adopted the override are unaffected
- **Verified in Chromium against a routed version file, four cases, zero page errors in all:** **(A)** file matches the document → **0** reloads, proving normalisation; **(B)** file ahead of the document → exactly **1** corrective reload with the guard key set; **(C)** file *permanently* ahead across **26 s / two full poll cycles** → still exactly **1** reload, proving the guard holds rather than merely not having polled yet; **(D)** meta tag stripped from the served HTML → **0** reloads, check disabled
- **Scope is one page.** `ovReloadFresh` exists only in `Profiler.html` and not in `live-site-pages/templates/`, so [PC-TEMPLATE-PROP] #19 does not fire and the other nine pages are untouched. They keep the pre-existing behaviour, and the amended rules say so explicitly rather than implying the corpus changed
- **`Sections: 98/100`** — **87 non-exempt** after excluding the eleven sections dated today, so no rotation was due. The page changelog stands at 51 total / **49 non-exempt** against its cap of 50, so no page rotation was due either

### Worth noting

- **THE NASDAQ QUESTION IS NOT MERELY UNANNOUNCED — IT IS ARITHMETICALLY UNRESOLVABLE, AND THAT IS A STRONGER ANSWER THAN THE BRIEF ASKED FOR.** The cure test is a closing bid at or above US$1.00 for **ten consecutive business days**. The ADS ratio change that raised the price took effect **2026-08-27**. Counting business days from there, with the US market closed on Monday **2026-09-07** for Labor Day, the tenth session cannot fall before **Thursday 2026-09-10** — one day after authoring. **Eight qualifying closes had completed** (2026-08-27 to 2026-09-08). So even on the most favourable assumption — at or above US$1.00 from the first post-ratio session and unbroken since — compliance could not have been confirmed on 2026-09-09, and the deadline is Monday 2026-09-14, a margin of **two business days**. No announcement exists either: EDGAR's newest NOVONIX filing of any kind is the 2026-09-02 6-K, and the ASX announcement list ends 2026-09-04
- **NOVONIX IS BEING REMOVED FROM THE S&P/ASX 300, AND NEITHER RESEARCH AGENT FOUND IT.** The S&P Dow Jones Indices announcement of **2026-09-04** — the company's single most recent announcement of any kind — lists NVX under Removal in the S&P/ASX 300 section, effective **before the open on Monday 2026-09-21**. The third-party agent reported it as commentary it could not confirm against the primary source (403) and the first-party agent could not extract the PDF's text. It was read here directly from `ir.novonixgroup.com/static-files/…` by decompressing the PDF content streams. Forced index selling lands in the same fortnight as the Nasdaq deadline, with the share price already below the Yorkville floor
- **The AD/CVD case NOVONIX petitioned for FAILED, and it did more damage than the Nasdaq notice six days later.** Commerce set final duties on 2026-02-13 — 93.5% antidumping, 102.72% China-wide, 66.82–66.86% countervailing, above 160% stacked — and the company publicised them. Every rate was conditional on an injury finding that never came. The consequence is priced in NOVONIX's own June impairment model, which cut the assumed realised selling price from **US$10–12/kg to US$7–11/kg** naming the Commerce ruling, against operating costs of US$6–8/kg. A 20–30% cut to the assumed price of the only product a company makes is a different business plan
- **Riverside's full-capacity date slipped from December 2027 to SEPTEMBER 2031** — nearly four years, disclosed in HY-2026 Note 6 as a conscious capital-conservation decision, and it now sits against a Section 48C condition that the first 11,000 tpa be in service **before 2028-04-07** or a certified US$103m credit lapses. Conserving cash can cost more than it saves
- **Three more NOVONIX findings no row carried.** The **Harper exclusive furnace licence lapsed on 2026-01-01** because the required initial payment was not made by 2025-12-31 — the 2020 supply agreement survives, so the company buys furnaces rather than holding the right to build them. The **Stellantis offtake was terminated 2025-11-04** over qualification specifications, removing the largest volume it ever held (86,250–115,000 t). And the **LGES convertible notes mandatorily convert on acceptance of a first purchase order and remain outstanding**, from which it follows that no purchase order has ever been accepted — an inference from the instrument's own terms, recorded as such
- **The Amortization Event trigger is more precise, and less alarming, than the brief stated.** Read directly from HY-2026 Note 8: five of seven consecutive trading days below the A$0.12 floor triggers a cash redemption of **20% of the US$35.0m** — about US$7.0m — recurring at 30-day intervals, not the whole balance at once. The floor was breached on the authoring date, at about A$0.098. The third-party agent discarded the five-of-seven formulation as belonging to an unrelated issuer; it is in NOVONIX's own filing, and the primary source was used over the agent's verdict
- **ALBEMARLE ANNOUNCED A CEO SUCCESSION SIX DAYS BEFORE THE IDENTITY SWEEP AND SEVEN BEFORE AUTHORING** — on 2026-09-02 the board appointed **Ragnar Udd**, BHP's Chief Commercial Officer, to succeed J. Kent Masters effective 2027-02-01 or earlier, with Masters becoming Executive Chair (8-K filed 2026-09-03). The 2026-09-09 batch identity sweep, which was correct on every other cell, missed it. This is exactly the case step 1a exists for, and it argues that a sweep dated the same day as authoring is still not a substitute for checking the filing index
- **The §8 row's framing of Albemarle as a distressed company divesting toward a pure-play is now wrong in both halves.** H1-2026 net income attributable was **US$799.1m** against a US$510.6m FY2025 loss; Q2-2026 adjusted EBITDA rose 155% to US$858.1m at a 49% margin, and about US$1.3bn of gross debt was retired, taking Moody's from a negative outlook to **stable** on leverage of 1.8x against 4.5x. And the divestitures were done **at a loss** — a US$95.0m pre-tax loss on Refining Solutions after US$181.1m of goodwill and US$245.6m of long-lived-asset impairments taken specifically to write it down to its sale price, against a US$42.3m Eurecat gain — with **neither qualifying as a discontinued operation** because management did not consider them a strategic shift. What they bought was the balance sheet
- **Two clean negatives on Albemarle, both worth recording because they are the kind a summary invents.** It **does not disclose claiming the Section 45X credit** — neither "Section 45X" nor "advanced manufacturing production credit" appears in the FY2025 10-K or the Q2-2026 10-Q — and it discloses **no named offtake or supply agreement with any cell maker or automaker**; Tesla appears only as a named competitor, which is the sole `relationships[]` entry the dossier can honestly carry
- **The Albemarle segment count changed and the FY2025 10-K is now misleading on it.** The Q2-2026 10-Q reports **two** reportable segments, Energy Storage and Specialties; the retained Performance Catalyst Solutions sits in "Corporate and All Other", which the filing says "is not considered to be a segment". The FY2025 10-K, four months older, still shows three
- **`check-source-reachability.py` was wrong about the SEC for the THIRD consecutive session, and the cause is now identified.** It is not a network block — it is a **User-Agent format failure**. SEC hosts return 403 to a UA without a contact email and 200 to one containing it; `LightAISolutions Research jonyang92@gmail.com` returned 200 on three consecutive attempts against both `sec.gov` and `data.sec.gov`, and the entire first-party financial record for both companies came from them. Also recovered: `ir.novonixgroup.com`, which both research agents recorded as 403, served 200 here and carries both the announcement list and the announcement PDFs
- **The P4 indent trap was neutralised by a read-detect-write helper rather than by memory.** It was proved byte-identical on a round-trip of all five shared JSON files before any write. The resulting diffs are 60 lines on the roster and 34 on the calendar, against the 2,350-line spurious diff P4 had to revert. - **S3 P3 · group E1a — the `stem` dossier (`profileVersion` 1, schema v7, category `supplier`, 90 sources, 80% first-party) and its schema-v2 study guide (16 sections).** Stem, Inc. (NYSE: STEM, CIK 1758766) is the corpus's first listed pure-play energy-optimisation software company. Research ran as two parallel `general-purpose` subagents — Stage 1 first-party exhaustive, Stage 2 third-party — over the FY2025 10-K, the Q2-2026 10-Q, the full IR press-release archive and the product pages, plus trade press, analyst panels and ranking sources
- **`repository-information/study-prep/stem/stem-lesson-plan.md`** — nine-module technology curriculum from the sensor to the market bid: the measurement chain and why point maps rather than algorithms are the barrier to entry, performance ratio against availability and six loss signatures, the control ladder from cell to fleet, what a power plant controller does and why it is specified in cycles, the revenue stack and the one-megawatt-hour-twice constraint, warranty-aware dispatch, hybridisation against a shared connection limit, and the bundled-against-independent split that defines the segment
- **Fourteen shared concepts registered** in `profiler-concepts.json` (1,311 → 1,325): asset performance management, performance ratio, clipping, soiling, data logger, point map, hardware-agnostic, annual recurring revenue, forecast error, gate closure, frequency containment reserve, OPC-UA, DNP 3.0 and imbalance charge

### Changed

- **README tree** — three entries added (profile, study guide, study-prep directory); README timestamp and repo version updated

### Fixed

- **A first-party date error in Stem's own FY2025 10-K, resolved against the filing.** The 10-K states the Athena → PowerTrack Optimizer rebrand was announced "In September 2024". Stem's press release is datelined **5 September 2025**, says "today announced the rebranding", launches the refreshed website "live on Sept. 5", and quotes a chief executive who did not take office until January 2025. The dossier uses September 2025 and records the filing's date as wrong, with the three grounds stated
- **Two research-brief premises corrected by the research itself** — there was **no 2024 revenue restatement** (an in-period variable-consideration reduction of $38.7m on hardware price guarantees, plus a $104.1m bad-debt charge; ICFR effective, no material weakness), and there is **no going-concern qualification** at either the FY2025 10-K or the Q2-2026 10-Q
- **A stale-by-one-cycle finding the batch identity check could not have caught:** Deloitte was dismissed on **2026-03-12** and RSM US LLP engaged, five days after the 10-K was filed, with no disagreements or reportable events. The de-SPAC securities class action was **dismissed with prejudice on 2025-12-17**

### Notes

- **What the record actually shows, and why the top line inverts it.** Total revenue fell $461.5m (FY2023) → $156.3m (FY2025) solely because battery hardware resale collapsed 96% to $0.286m in H1 2026; over the same period the software, edge and services core grew $76.0m → $111.2m, GAAP gross margin swung −8% → 38%, and adjusted EBITDA turned positive at $6.7m then $8.2m. Set against that, the balance sheet has not recovered: a $270.0m stockholders' deficit, $38.4m of cash against $18.4m of cash interest paid in the half, and the company **electing to pay its 12% secured notes in kind** from 2026-01-01 rather than 11% in cash
- **The incumbent typing is deliberately narrow.** Stem appears on **none** of the 2024 Guidehouse VPP, Grid DERMS or grid-edge DERMS leaderboards, nor S&P Global's 2026 Tier 1 cleantech list; Modo Energy's 2025 US optimizer directory lists it in capability-only language while quantifying performance for six peers. Its only ranking is Guidehouse's **2022** first place for AlsoEnergy in solar and storage monitoring, and its only contemporary recognition is **The smarter E AWARD 2026** for PowerTrack EMS. Wood Mackenzie's "Global BESS software landscape 2026" is paywalled and is named in the dossier as the collection gap that would settle it
- **A clean negative recorded as a finding:** Stem has no data-centre product, contract or revenue. The only mention located anywhere is one exploratory sentence by the chief executive on the Q1 2026 call, not repeated on the Q2 call. AIONA (launched 2026-06-17) is AI consulting sold to energy organisations, not a data-centre offering
- **Corpus reconciliation — 6 inbound files reviewed, 0 revised, and the reasoning recorded.** `available-power` and `excelsior-energy-capital` carry accurate 2022-dated claims, both now curated as `relationships[]` with their unresolved status stated rather than inferred; `enchanted-rock`'s mention is an executive career history (its CEO is Stem's former CEO); `sungrow` is a false positive ("Stem Cell Grid-Forming" is Sungrow's own technology name). `dnv` and `kwh-analytics` both name **"Also Energy"** — Stem's own subsidiary since February 2022 — as an independent platform. Those claims are **accurate as written**, so neither dossier was revised; instead the alias was registered in `aka[]` so the next reconciliation finds the connection by search rather than by luck
- **An environment finding for future sessions:** `check-source-reachability.py` reported `sec.gov` and `data.sec.gov` **BLOCKED**, but a direct probe with a declared non-personal User-Agent returned **200** on EDGAR company search, filing indexes and full 10-K/10-Q documents, and the entire first-party financial record here came from them. The roster's verdict bounded the roster's URL, not the filing route — §7's own "probe the hosts the session actually needs" rule, confirmed from the other direction. `businesswire.com` **is** genuinely blocked (403 on three attempts), and every affected citation was substituted with the identical release on `investors.stem.com`, which is a better first-party tier anyway
- **Verification:** `sync-profiler-registry.py --check` clean with the roster/calendar bijection at 161; `build-profiler-graph.py` rebuilt to 1,321 edges; `check-profiler-study.py` clean over 161 guides and 1,325 concepts; `check-profiler-relationships.py` 0 findings; `check-profiler-crossrefs.py` 0 candidates. Playwright against a local server rendered the dossier and the study guide with **zero console errors, zero page errors** and all six headshots loading; Key Personnel, Relationships and Financial Reporting tabs all render. ### Added

- **`companies[].aka[]` — a registry field for every other name a company is known by** (PROFILER-SCHEMA.md, registry schemaVersion 2 → 3). Former names, the operating brand where it differs from the display name, short forms, native-script names and separately branded subsidiaries. It is mechanical and unrendered, exactly like `domains`: nothing in `Profiler.html` reads it, and its only consumer is the corpus-reconciliation grep in Profiler Command step 7, which now searches a **recorded** list instead of a guessed one
- **A "Naming and renames" section in `PROFILER-SCHEMA.md`** setting out four fields with four jobs — `slug` never changes, `name` is the legal name, `shortName` / registry `name` is the display name, `aka[]` grows forever — plus a **collision test** for choosing a display name on a rebrand: default to the name the market uses, override it when the new brand is a common word, a surname, or a string already meaning something else in the corpus, and `grep -rE '\b<NewName>\b'` before deciding
- **Profiler Command step 1a — mandatory identity verification before research.** Ticker and exchange read off a filing cover or exchange notice; legal name against operating brand; an eighteen-month search for a change of control; confirmation the registrant exists as assumed; and a requirement to **correct the source you were working from** in the same commit

### Changed

- **`aka[]` populated for five companies.** `marsh-mclennan` (15 entries) and `aon` (8) from this session's research; and a bounded backfill of the only three companies in the corpus whose legal name diverges materially from their display name — `amd` (Advanced Micro Devices), `catl` (Contemporary Amperex Technology) and `supermicro` (Super Micro Computer). No mass migration: the schema's opportunistic-backfill rule stands, and step 1a now populates the field at authoring
- **`PROFILER-COVERAGE-PLAN.md` §8 preamble** now warns that the identity cells are a 2026-09-07 snapshot rather than facts, names the three consecutive sessions in which they were wrong, and points at step 1a as the mechanism
- **`CLASSROOM-CURRICULUM-PLAN.md` §10.4** gained three findings on segment-lesson regeneration (below)

### Fixed

- **The display-name question is resolved structurally rather than by picking a label, and the label was never the systematic risk.** The risk is that corpus reconciliation greps a display name and misses inbound claims. Measured on this corpus: grepping `Marsh McLennan` returns **two** dossiers; grepping the `aka[]` set returns **six**, and the extras include **`dnv`** — which carries the most important inbound claim about the company, that its battery guidance names no certifier — reachable only through the alias `Marsh`, plus `csa-group` reachable only through `Oliver Wyman`. A session reconciling on the display name alone would have missed both. The display name stays "Marsh McLennan" because "Marsh" fails the collision test twice over (Clearway's Marsh Landing generating station, Entergy's chief executive Drew Marsh)
- **`kwh-analytics` registry `lastUpdated` drift**, caught by `sync-profiler-registry.py`: the entry still read 2026-09-08 after v05.22r bumped the profile to profileVersion 2 on 2026-09-09. Now synced; the roster's coverage line was stale for one commit

### Worth noting

- **A correction to what v05.22r's response told the developer: it is not three segment lessons queued, it is all nineteen.** `build-classroom-segments.py --check` reports `19 segment(s), 19 due`. On the wrong reading nobody owns it, because S1 is closed and S2 authors a different artefact. **The right home is per segment inside its own S2 session** — §10.6 requires the segment lesson's `read-next` to deep-link to the landscape module, so the lesson must be regenerated *after* the module exists. One `--segment <id>` run per S2 session, inside the pipeline checker's caps

### Notes

- **The worked example is the credit-substitution chain, taught only from what the four fetched records state.** One tenant of record (Fluidstack), two landlords (Hut 8, TeraWulf) and one backstop (Google), and the graph types the same arrangement three ways: Fluidstack types both landlords **supplier**, Hut 8 types Fluidstack **customer** (`project` `river-bend-campus`, the only project pin on the pair), and Hut 8 types TeraWulf **competitor** — because from where Hut 8 stands the other landlord is the comparable, not a counterparty. TeraWulf curates five relationships and its largest tenant is not among them, so the Fluidstack–TeraWulf edge is fully stated from one end only. The backstop is the sharper case: Google's dossier types Fluidstack, Hut 8 and TeraWulf all **partner**, while TeraWulf's own dossier types Google **investor** — the support there was bought with penny warrants; Hut 8's records no warrants and no equity for the same instrument. Google's dossier also types Cipher Digital **portfolio**, the coherent inverse of the **investor** Cipher states, so the corpus holds both the corrected and the uncorrected form of the identical pair. Per the content contract this is a **mechanism** lesson: the chain is a worked example and no card or quiz item asks for a company fact — every one of the 8 flashcards and 5 quiz items is about the data shape
- **`where-it-fails` covers four misreads, each built on those same records**, satisfying and exceeding the three §10.5 requires: (1) *a cross-mention read as a deal* — Hut 8's dossier names TeraWulf five times and every one is a benchmark sentence, none of the five carries a date, and the edge's `last` is consequently empty; (2) *a `partner` taken at face value* — the partner/investor split above; (3) *one counterparty read as "the customer"* — Hut 8 types **both** Fluidstack (tenant of record) and Anthropic (end user) `customer` at the same campus; (4) *`status` read as current* — Fluidstack's links to Entergy and AEP are `announced`, not `active`, and the Fluidstack–TeraWulf note records a July 2026 change of substance under an unchanged `supplier` type and an unchanged `since`

### Fixed

- **BYD's specified-foreign-entity status resolved and written into the corpus** (`byd` v8 → **v9**, v8 archived). The dossier's `policyExposure[]` entry for the OBBBA §45X/§48E regime carried "UNRESOLVED, AND RECORDED AS SUCH" with a `mitigation` reading "Not determinable until the SFE question is closed against Notice 2026-15 itself." It is now closed against that notice, read in full (95pp, fetched from irs.gov): §7701(a)(51)(B) reaches both entities named in **NDAA FY2024 §154(b)** and entities on the **§1260H** list, and BYD is on both — two independent sufficient prongs.
- **The judgment prose reordered so the load-bearing instrument leads.** `strategyRead` previously framed the June 2026 §1260H listing as the sharper US item. For §45X/§48E purposes it is the §154(b) prong that carries the **first** taxable year: Notice 2026-15's footnote 21 places the determination for the first taxable year beginning after 4 July 2025 at the **first day** of that year — 1 January 2026 for a calendar-year filer, five months before the §1260H addition. From the second year the last-day rule applies and both prongs bite.
- **The exposure is stated as a ratio test rather than a ban**, with the arithmetic and the grandfather both recorded: 55% non-prohibited content for an energy storage technology beginning construction in calendar 2026 rising toward 75% by 2030, and the §7701(a)(52)(D)(iv) carve-out excluding costs under binding written contracts entered into before 16 June 2025 from the material assistance cost ratio entirely.
- **Two traps in the notice recorded so a later reader does not fall into them**: it publishes **no list** of specified foreign entities — §3.01(5)(b) puts the determination on the taxpayer — and its two "as in effect on January 1, 2025" clauses attach to the **beginning-of-construction** rules, not to the SFE lists, which are referenced dynamically.
- **`byd`'s refresh-calendar `watch[]` line flipped from open question to settled finding**, redirected at Treasury's forthcoming proposed PFE regulations and the anti-circumvention rules the notice signals.

Measured against the live tree: the full shape for 1,920 items is ~146 KB — past the 100 KB `CacheService` value cap the §10.7 arithmetic assumed it would stay under — and would have fallen through to an uncached rebuild on every request; the compact form is ~61 KB. ### Changed

- **Phase 2b of the integrated remediation plan — F9, the `tract` ↔ `prime-data-centers` citation read. No new research; every cited page on both sides was opened and the scope of each Lockhart figure established. The hypothesis was tested, not assumed, and it held with one correction: the USD 4bn is not an eight-building total — it is a rounding of an older, nine-building one.**
  - **What the sources say.** The Muskin Elam page behind `tract`'s USD 4bn is **dated** (`datePublished` 2025-05-08) and is a reprint of Justin Sayers's Austin Business Journal report on the Tract acquisition ("Originally written by Justin Sayers for the Austin Business Journal"); its words are "across the street from a $4 billion data center campus project by Dallas-based Prime Data Centers LLC" — **no scope stated**. The primary behind that rounding is the **Caldwell County Commissioners Court special meeting of 12 March 2024** (Hays Caldwell EDP 2024-03-13, Post Register 2024-03-20, repeated verbatim by the Caldwell DCAT tracker — all three already in `prime-data-centers`'s `sources[]`): "Phase 1 of the development, known as Project Snow, will have a developer capital expenditure of **$1.3 billion for 3 buildings**. There will be a total of **nine data centers** on the site when all three phases are completed. The total capital expenditure at full buildout would be **$4.2 billion**." Texas filing **TABS2026019473** (registered 5 May 2026, read live) restates phase 1 as "Two new 380,000 sf shell buildings for future use as data centers", 760,000 sq ft, **estimated cost $400,000,000**, June 2026 to September 2027, owner Lockhart Property, LLC. Baxtel's Prime Austin page (read live) carries the current master plan — 206 acres, **eight buildings totalling 2 million sq ft**, phase 1 "provisionally pegged at $1.3 billion and 144 MW across three buildings" before the $400 million two-building filing. **So USD 4.2bn ≈ USD 4bn is the full three-phase, nine-building build-out of the 2024 plan; USD 1.3bn was that plan's turnkey phase 1; USD 400m is the two-shell phase 1 of the 2026 filing; and no source restates a full-build-out total for the eight-building / 384 MW plan on Prime's campus page.** Different scopes and different vintages, not a contradiction. Austin Monitor (`tract` sources[72]) and GovTech Insider ([73]) mention neither Prime nor any dollar figure; Baxtel's Caldwell Valley page ([146]) gives only the 1.43-mile distance. Prime's own campus page returned 403 directly and the Wayback Machine was unreachable from this session (connection reset), so the 205 acres / eight buildings / 384 MW stand on the dossier's earlier Wayback read, corroborated by Baxtel and the DCAT tracker (384 MW, 206 acres).
  - **`tract` v3 (v2 archived).** Nothing deleted. `relationships[2].note` no longer carries the bare "USD 4 billion"; the edge `context` now states both figures with their scopes, the ABJ attribution and the reconciliation; `productsAndServices[2].highlights[0]` states "USD 4.2 billion at full build-out on the county's March 2024 nine-building plan (rounded to 'USD 4 billion' in Tract-side press), USD 400 million for the two-shell first phase filed in May 2026". `sources[]`: the Muskin Elam entry **dated 2025-05-08, relabelled as the ABJ reprint with the quoted words and "no scope stated", and moved from the undated tail into chronological position** (between GovTech 2025-05-13 and the 2025-05-07 release mirror — the edge's `source` URL is unchanged); four sources added — Texas TDLR TABS2026019473 (2026-05-05), Post Register (2024-03-20), Hays Caldwell EDP (2024-03-13) and Baxtel's Prime Austin page (undated). `srcTotal` 151 → 155; `srcFirstPct` 34 → 33.
  - **`prime-data-centers` v3 (v2 archived) — step 7 in the other direction.** Prime's dossier cited the county announcement in three places but never stated its USD 4.2bn full-build-out figure, so the campus record read as if USD 1.3bn → USD 400m were the whole story. The real blind spot is structural: `detect_figures` compares *what A says about B* against *what B says about A*, and **never against what B says about itself** — which is exactly the record that contradicts tract's USD 4bn. Recommended in priority order: (1) an `A-on-B versus B-on-B` comparison behind a flag, calibrated before promotion; (2) `--no-cap` as an audit switch rather than a higher default; (3) leave the cap at 900.

### Added

- **Four schema-v2 study guides plus lesson plans, closing the §8 backfill ledger — no dossier touched.** `plus-power` (11 sections), `nextera-energy-resources` (11), `lightsource-bp` (11), `terra-gen` (11) — **44 sections**. No `profileVersion` moved, nothing archived, `archive-index.json` untouched, `profiler-companies.json` never opened, and `profiler-graph.json` came back **byte-identical** after the rebuild — asserted against the specific expected digest rather than against "same as before" (sha256 `563ae281f00ce164075ded5c21315c67769bbf50bcb8a8d046c80b882c2168d6`, now unchanged across **four** consecutive guides-only sessions) and correctly absent from the diff. The corpus reaches **154 companies, 154 guides — every dossier now has one.**
- **The collision field was far larger than the brief described, and finding that out changed the syllabus.** The brief named five session-1 guides to read. Reading them was necessary but not sufficient: a scan of all 150 existing guides found **fourteen more owner-side guides** already covering the ground the brief proposed for the portfolio-finance topic — `engie-north-america` owns *Capital recycling: how you fund the next battery when the last one earns less*, `clearway-energy` owns the yieldco, CAFD and tax-equity/HLBV, `recurrent-energy`, `spearmint-energy`, `strata-clean-energy`, `available-power` and `aes-clean-energy` all own develop-and-sell in some form, `aypa-power` and `gridstor` own the financing stack and the sponsor exit, `invenergy` owns who-owns-a-private-developer, and **`rwe-clean-energy` owns repowering**. The brief's candidate list — fleet building, portfolio financing, the regulated affiliate, fleet operations, international origination — was therefore **five topics of which one was already shipped and one was partly shipped**. Portfolio financing was dropped as a standalone topic and its one genuinely unclaimed part, the regulated-utility affiliate, was merged into the scale guide; repowering was dropped entirely.
- **What survived the triage was four non-overlapping topics, and the course was built around one claim: at portfolio scale the unit of production is a process, and every practice is a way of managing an obligation that outlives every contract that created it.**
  - `plus-power` — *the process, not the plant*: a **reference design as frozen decisions**, with the rule that separates what a fleet may freeze from what it may not — *decisions freeze, measurements do not* — and the failure mode of letting a measurement quietly become an assumption; why repetition is worth more in **permitting, lending and insurance** than in procurement, because those are examinations of an argument rather than purchases of a thing; the same block on five networks (island grid-forming with black start, weak-point damping and operator model validation, winter duty, desert derating, dense-urban fire footprint) and the observation that **none of it is a different battery**; the serial build curve, why it flattens well above zero and why it **resets at a border**; the owner's build-organisation decision, read as the contractor course's bargain from the other chair; **concurrency rather than capital** as the growth cap, with warranty clocks that start at delivery so a finished unenergised plant spends guarantee life it will never earn against; a pipeline as a portfolio of options whose discipline is killing them early; and the **replicated defect** — the only failure in the course that cannot exist above one project, deliberately distinguished from the contracting guide's supplier-diversity argument because one is about a company and the other about a fault
  - `nextera-energy-resources` — *the factory's calendar*: built on the observation that **two rules the first course states as laws are facts about being small**. Self-integration, with access to scarce cell capacity rather than margin as the motive, and the control layer held rather than escrowed; the **six seams** that change owner when nobody sold you a system, ending on the honest summary that one counterparty is worth a great deal when something is wrong and nothing at all if it is thin; forward booking as the purchase of a **calendar**, converting procurement into a position with inventory, working-capital and obsolescence consequences — and the odd result that a long owner sells equipment to its competitors; the long-lead list, where the two slowest items are ordinary grid equipment contested by every other connection on the network; the **regulated affiliate**, what it buys (credit, permanence, tax capacity, shared scale) and the three costs usually omitted (enforced separation and arm's-length dealing, the regulated side's risks entering the group's credit, and competing for capital against a rate base with a better argument); the **tax-appetite inversion**, which moves the eligibility gate inside the company; one battery under three owners, explicitly distinguished from the dispatch guide's version because that one is skill and this one is discount rate; and large owner against specialist, noting that several of the large owner's advantages are **positional rather than earned**
  - `lightsource-bp` — *what crosses a border*: the first international guide in the corpus, and the first project developer outside the US it holds. Six support instruments read as what each fixes, leaves floating and therefore rewards, with the financing consequence read backwards — **policy design rather than engineering sets much of what a country's electricity costs**; the national auction as an institution, with prequalification spend committed before the outcome is known, delivery security that converts a win into an obligation, **indexation frequently worth more than the headline price**, and the winner's curse as a property of the format rather than a bidder's error; the currency problem, asymmetric in time and unhedgeable at the tenor that matters, with local-currency borrowing named as the honest and least-used answer; what a network rulebook can decide, including per-connection loss factors that move revenue administratively with nothing happening at the plant; consent risk as **variance rather than difficulty**; the portable/non-portable split, with the finding that portable capability is capital-like and non-portable capability is time-like — which is why entry is usually an acquisition and why the price looks high against the assets; and a three-part reading of diversification — **effective against politics, weak against finance and supply chains, useless against a design fault** — including the counter-intuitive point that a portfolio of long fixed-price contracts is *more* exposed to interest rates than a merchant one, because a fixed strike price is a bond
  - `terra-gen` — *the twenty years after the ribbon*, and the **close of the buyer-side course**: asset management as commercial custody rather than maintenance, the layer that scales worst because a contractor can be paid to perform an obligation but never made liable for one, and is underfunded because its output when working is the absence of a surprise; **four different things called availability**, with the excluded-event list identified as where the negotiation actually lives and a fleet average identified as a reporting convenience rather than exposure; **enforcing** a guarantee — a demonstration costing a day out of the market on a schedule the contract rather than the equipment decides, records without which the supplier's first defence stands, notice deadlines as the commonest way a sound claim is lost, and a remedy ladder whose escalation mechanism is worth more than its rungs; augmentation as a **date** that must be designed into a site years ahead, with the vintage-mixing constraint; the operating bill for a mixed-vendor fleet, which is where the contracting guide's diversity argument is actually paid; fleet telemetry from the owner's side, where twenty-nine normal sites are the control group that turns an anomaly into **evidence rather than an argument**; self-perform against a wrap that runs half the asset's life; and the closing claim that every other party in the chain sells something with a term, so the owner-operator is defined by holding what is left — which is why assets change hands after commercial operation, and is usually the system working
- **Seven concepts registered** (1,203 → 1,210), and the ledger was the checker's rather than the author's: **no definition was written until `check-profiler-study.py` had proved the term referenced**, which is session 4's inverted order kept deliberately. `bid-bond`, `contract-for-difference`, `corporate-ppa`, `feed-in-premium`, `feed-in-tariff`, `green-certificate`, `marginal-loss-factor` — all seven from the `lightsource-bp` guide; the other three guides needed **zero** new concepts, which is itself a measure of how deep the registry now is on project finance, contracting and grid vocabulary.

### Changed

- **`PROFILER-COVERAGE-PLAN.md` §8 — the study-guide backfill ledger is closed.** The four rows flipped to `✓ · v05.00r`, and the section now carries **zero** outstanding `— ` cells. Session 6 has no backfill rows left, so the ledger that opened at v04.51r is complete.
- **`README.md`** — twelve tree lines added: four `<slug>.study.json` entries in the `profiler-data/` listing and four `study-prep/<slug>/` directory nodes each with its lesson plan.

### Notes

- **The brief's central CHANGELOG premise was wrong, and the reason is worth recording because it will recur.** It stated that rotation "FIRES on your push — unavoidable, not merely likely", reasoning from 99 sections plus one to reach the 100 trigger. The counter was verified against the real count as instructed — `grep -c '^## \[v[0-9]'` returned **99**, matching `Sections: 99/100` — but the arithmetic omits the **current-day exemption**. `v04.99r` is dated **2026-09-07**, the same EST day as this push, so with this section added the file holds 100 sections of which **two are today's**, leaving **98 non-exempt**. Rotation does **not** fire. Five of the six worked scenarios in `CHANGELOG-archive.md` state the threshold explicitly against the non-exempt count — Scenario A ("99 < 100 → no rotation needed (the threshold counts non-exempt only, so a total above 100 does not by itself trigger)") and Scenario D ("97 < 100 → no rotation needed **despite 102 total**") are decisive, and C, E and F all re-check against non-exempt.
- **A second, residual contradiction was found in the same document — recorded, not fixed.** The v04.98r reconciliation harmonised the *threshold* (99-or-fewer stops; exactly 100 rotates) but left the *basis* inconsistent: **steps 1–2 count all sections** and **step 4** then mandates at least one rotation once that check trips, while **Scenarios A and D** say a total at or above 100 does not trigger when the non-exempt count is below it. Applied to this session the two readings disagree — steps 1–2 and 4 would have rotated thirteen sections that the scenarios say must stay. The scenarios were followed, on the strength of being five consistent worked examples against one shorthand, and because they are the only place the exemption's interaction with the threshold is actually exercised. **The failure modes are opposite and both real:** the v04.98r contradiction caused rotations to be *skipped* (file grows unboundedly), this one causes them to fire *early* (a date group leaves sooner than the rule intends). The suggested fix is a one-line change to step 1 — count **non-exempt** sections, since step 3's exemption is applied before any threshold test in every scenario — plus matching wording in step 2 and the quick rule. **Not done here:** this session's scope is study guides, and editing rotation logic on a guides push is the kind of scope widening that produces its own errors.
- **Rotation is genuinely due next session, and the counter alone will not say so.** The counter now reads `Sections: 100/100`, which looks like a rotation that was skipped. It was not. On the next push, on a later EST day, the total becomes 101 with **one** exempt section, leaving **100 non-exempt** — at the trigger. The oldest whole date group is **thirteen sections dated 2026-09-01** (`v04.01r` through `v04.13r`). The clone arrives shallow at ~50 commits and `git fetch --unshallow` **must** run before any SHA lookup; it was run at the start of this session (51 → 1,005 commits) purely so that the rotation, had it fired, would have enriched correctly.
- **All six checkers exit 0**, with the roster/refresh-calendar bijection clean at **154**: `sync-profiler-registry.py --check` (0 of 154 out of sync), `check-profiler-study.py` (154 guides + 1,210 concepts, 0 errors 0 warnings), `check-profiler-relationships.py` (0 findings, 10 pre-existing accept-list suppressions), `check-profiler-crossrefs.py`, `check-profiler-reports.py` (0 errors, 38 pre-existing aged-pin warnings — warning-only by design, X3's close-out), and `build-profiler-graph.py`.
- **The `[PC-README-TREE]` completeness audit came back exact on all three listings, confirming session 4's figures and finding no new drift.** `live-site-pages/profiler-data/` 312 files, all listed (the two extra README matches are the `archive/` and `reports/` directory nodes); `study-prep/` **106 directories** and **131 files**, all listed, with nothing listed that is not on disk. The pre-existing cosmetics recorded at v04.98r were left as recorded: ordering breaks in the `study-prep/` listing, the `rolls-royce-power-systems` plan nested under `megmeet/`, and raw `&` in existing `Samsung C&T` entries.
- **Two abbreviations were deliberately left unregistered, with the reason recorded so a future session does not re-derive it.** Concept matching is exact-lowercase, so registering `CfD` as an alias would also claim **CFD** — computational fluid dynamics — in a corpus that holds `aligned`, `coolit`, `canadian-solar` and several other thermal guides; and registering `FIT` would claim **failures in time**, the standard reliability unit, in a corpus that holds `infineon`, `vicor` and `delta-electronics`. Neither abbreviation is used by any guide today, so neither was needed. Whoever needs one first should take it knowingly.
- **Marker placement was asserted programmatically again, with the v04.99r rule encoded correctly this time** — a shared helper refused to write any guide carrying `{{…}}` in `title`, `read` or `timeline.lanes.*`, or inside a quiz **choice**, while permitting markers in quiz `q` and `why`. All four guides passed first time. Separately, `bars` item labels were verified as marker-safe by **reading the renderer** rather than by precedent: `gdBars` (`live-site-pages/Profiler.html:7238`) passes both `label` and `sub` through `gdFmt` and uses `gdPlain` for the bar's own hover title, so a term in a bar label renders its tooltip and is not a `<button>` — the trap that disqualifies quiz choices does not apply.
- **JSON conventions were round-trip asserted per file.** The four guides at `indent=1`, `ensure_ascii=False`, trailing newline; the concepts registry at `indent=2`. The concepts merge was asserted a **pure insertion** by filtering the seven new slugs out of the post-merge slug list and requiring byte-order equality with the pre-merge list — the seven landed at indices 87, 215, 226, 425, 426, 501 and 673 with no existing entry moved.

## [v04.99r] — 2026-09-07 12:52:38 AM EST — v01.83w

> **Prompt:** "Regarding the blackstone/Macquarie quiz marker question, where can I check the quiz rendering path
> to settle whether markers there format or show literal braces? Are you referring to the self test section of the
> study guide?" … "continue with your recommendation"

### Fixed

- **The three-session-old `blackstone`/`macquarie` quiz-marker question is settled, and the answer reverses the assumption it was filed under.** The suspicion, carried forward unexamined since v04.95r, was that `{{term}}` markers inside `check-yourself` quiz items might render as literal braces. **They do not.** `gdQuiz` (`live-site-pages/Profiler.html:7274`) passes the question, **every choice**, and the `why` explanation through `gdFmt` (`:6714`), which is the same formatter every other authored field uses; `gdBindTermTips` (`:7307`) binds by **event delegation on the guide shell** (`:7098`), checking `ev.target.classList.contains('gd-term')`, so a term span nested inside a `<button>` is reached like any other. The hand-enforced "keep quiz items marker-free" convention was **stricter than the renderer requires**, and the real scope was never a class of breakage: **three markers total** — `blackstone` had one in a choice and one in `why`, `macquarie` one in `why` — all three resolving to real registry definitions.
- **But quiz *choices* are a genuine trap, for a reason that has nothing to do with rendering — measured, not inferred.** A standalone headless-Chromium reproduction of the exact markup and listeners returned: hovering a term in an **enabled** choice fires the tooltip; **clicking** it logs `ANSWERED` *before* `TIPCLICK`, because the term span is a child of the button and the click bubbles to the button's own handler — so on a touch device there is no way to read the definition without committing that answer; and after `gdQuiz` sets `button.disabled`, hovering the term returns **nothing**, because disabled form controls suppress mouse events on their descendants — so the tooltip dies exactly when a reader is reviewing a wrong answer. A marker in `q` or in `why` has neither problem: both are plain `<div>` containers.
- **`scripts/check-profiler-study.py` now enforces exactly that boundary.** A new guard in `check_unformatted()` errors on `{{…}}` inside `quiz items[].c[]` and deliberately leaves `q` and `why` **unguarded**. The comment records why this case is unlike its two neighbours in that function: `title`, `read` and `timeline.lanes.*` are literal-braces bugs, whereas a quiz choice renders correctly and fails on interaction. Verified both ways — the guard reported exactly **one** error across all 150 guides before the fix (`blackstone.study.json sections[10] items[0].c[1]`), confirming the corpus-wide scope, and zero after it.
- **`blackstone.study.json`** — `{{fee-earning AUM}}` moved out of the choice text (now reading "not yet fee-earning capital") and into that item's `why`, which already referenced the same term in plain text. The tooltip survives, both wrinkles are gone, and `{{dry powder}}` beside it is untouched. `lastUpdated` → 2026-09-07. **`macquarie.study.json` was not modified** — its single marker is in `why`, where the behaviour is correct.

### Changed

- **`SESSION-CONTEXT.md` standing-item list** — the quiz-marker question and the CHANGELOG rotation-rule contradiction both removed, with a note recording where each was settled (v04.99r and v04.98r). Three items remain: the README archive listing, the missing `archive/nvidia.profile.v2.json`, and the two network-blocked research hosts.

### Notes

- **The session's own authoring helper had encoded the old rule, and caught itself doing it.** The scratchpad audit used while writing the four EPC guides refused any marker anywhere in a quiz item, so it rejected the corrected `blackstone` file — the legitimate `why` marker tripped it. That is a small, clean demonstration of the finding: the blanket convention was wrong, and a tool built from it produces false positives. The helper was narrowed to choices before the edit was applied.
- **The four v04.98r EPC guides are unaffected** — they keep all quiz items marker-free, which is conservative but valid under the refined rule. No guide was rewritten to add markers; the rule is a guard, not a prescription.
- **No dossier, concept or graph change** — this touches one study guide, one checker and three bookkeeping files. `profiler-companies.json` and `profiler-graph.json` were not opened.
